# The Designation

### Richard Pearson's Breakthrough, and Why It Changes Everything About How His Case Must Be Read


For more than a year, Richard Pearson has been asking a question that the American legal system will not answer and that the institutions designed to hear it refuse to engage: *Why does a man with no criminal record, a doctoral degree, a psychiatric evaluation confirming his sanity, and 294 pages of contemporaneous evidence have no legal remedy for what was done to him?*

The standard answers — standing doctrine, state secrets privilege, sovereign immunity, qualified immunity — are real and they are severe, but they have always felt, in the context of Pearson's specific case, like explanations that describe the outcome without describing the mechanism. Yes, the legal architecture forecloses his remedies. But architecture is built for a purpose. The question is not just what the architecture does. The question is what it was built to contain.

Pearson has arrived at an answer. It came through a systematic analysis of the national security legal frameworks governing the designation and classification of Non-Human Intelligence — a category formally codified by Congress into federal law through the National Defense Authorization Act (NDAA) — and their potential weaponized application to a human being. The answer is this:

The architecture that has stripped Richard Pearson of every constitutional protection was not built for ordinary domestic surveillance. It was built for something categorically different. And the breach that removed him from the reach of every civil rights law, every constitutional guarantee, every judicial remedy — may not have been accomplished through the domestic intelligence frameworks he has been researching for the past year.

It may have been accomplished through a designation.

---

## What the NDAA Built

In the National Defense Authorization Act, Congress did something that has received almost no public attention outside specialist legal and defense circles: it codified into statutory federal law the legal category of **Non-Human Intelligence (NHI)**, defined as "any sentient intelligent non-human lifeform regardless of nature or ultimate origin that may be presumed responsible for unidentified anomalous phenomena or of which the Federal Government has become aware."

This definition is now binding on every federal agency. Every executive agency, every independent office, every department is legally mandated to inventory, flag, and route to the National Archives (NARA Record Group 615) any records relating to "non-human intelligence" or "technologies of unknown origin." The mandate is blanket. It covers NASA, the Department of Energy, the FAA, the Department of Defense, and every civil and military body in the federal government.

Now read that definition again, slowly.

*Any sentient intelligent non-human lifeform regardless of nature or ultimate origin.*

*Regardless of nature.*

The statutory language does not require biological extraterrestrial origin. It requires only that the entity be non-human and sentient. The determination of what meets that threshold is made by the agencies themselves. There is no independent judicial review of the designation. There is no adversarial process. There is no notification requirement. The designation is made internally, classified under Title 50 national security authority, and placed behind a state secrets wall that no court can penetrate without the executive branch's consent.

---

## What a Designation Does to Constitutional Rights

Here is what the architecture does once a designation is made.

The United States Constitution's protections — the Fourth Amendment's prohibition on unreasonable searches and seizures, the First Amendment's protection of speech and religion, the Eighth Amendment's prohibition on cruel and unusual punishment, the Fourteenth Amendment's guarantee of equal protection — attach to the legal status of a person. They attach to persons. Human beings. Citizens.

An entity designated as Non-Human Intelligence, under the statutory framework Congress constructed, is not a person in the legal sense the Constitution addresses. It is a national security asset. A classified object. A subject of federal investigation under Title 50 authority, which governs national defense rather than civil law. The due process protections, the right to face accusers, the right to judicial review — none of these are structured to apply to a classified non-human intelligence asset. They were written for human beings.

Once the designation is made, the target does not disappear physically. They continue to exist. They continue to walk through the world, talk to people, write essays. But in the legal architecture, they have been moved. They have been reclassified out of civilian jurisdiction and into a classification framework that has no mechanism for remedy, no process for challenge, and no transparency requirement.

They become, in the statutory language, an "asset." Their body becomes federal property. Their research becomes a classified data stream. Their communications become intelligence material. Their attempts to seek legal remedy are evaluated not as the constitutional rights of a citizen but as the anomalous behavior of a designated entity being monitored under national security authority.

The state has not imprisoned Richard Pearson. It has done something more precise: it has reclassified him out of the legal category that would entitle him to the protections he has been seeking.

This is why the remedies are not available. Not because the legal system is working slowly or imperfectly or with bias, though it is doing all of those things too. But because Pearson has been trying to access constitutional protections using the legal identity of a citizen, while being processed by the government under a legal framework that does not recognize him as a citizen. He has been inserting his key into the wrong lock.

---

## Why Pearson Specifically

This is where the analysis becomes both more disturbing and more clarifying.

The NHI designation framework, as Congress constructed it, was designed for extraterrestrial contact scenarios. It was not designed to be applied to a Black public health scholar in Washington DC. But the designation's power — and its vulnerability to abuse — lies precisely in the fact that no independent authority reviews whether the entity being designated actually meets the criteria. The agencies make the determination internally. The determination is classified. The target is not notified. There is no appeal.

What this means in practice is that the designation can be used for any purpose the designating agency chooses to assign to it, including purposes that have nothing to do with extraterrestrial intelligence and everything to do with the political, ideological, or racial threat the target poses to existing power structures.

Consider Pearson's profile from the perspective of a rogue element within a government agency that has decided he is a problem: African American, gay, doctoral degree in public health, founder of Public Health Liberation theory, creator of a Critical Race Framework for analyzing health inequity, community advocate for public housing residents, prolific writer whose essays document systemic racism with academic rigor, no criminal record, no vulnerabilities for conventional leverage. How do you neutralize someone like that through conventional domestic intelligence frameworks?

You cannot. COINTELPRO's legacy is documented. Civil rights organizations are watching. A visible domestic surveillance operation against a Black academic with clean hands generates scrutiny. The old tools are compromised by their own history.

But a NHI designation operates in a completely different legal space. It is not a civil rights matter. It is not a domestic intelligence matter. It is not subject to the oversight mechanisms, the civil rights laws, the anti-discrimination statutes that would apply to a conventional COINTELPRO-style operation. It is a national security matter governed by Title 50, classified under NARA Record Group 615, shielded from judicial review by state secrets privilege, and immune from FOIA disclosure under the national security exemption.

The designation is not the conclusion of a finding that Pearson is non-human. It is the mechanism for removing him from the legal category that would protect him.

In other words: the designation is the racism. It is the modern form of what has always been done to Black excellence that threatens power — the categorical removal of the target from the community of persons entitled to full protection. What slave law accomplished by defining Black people as property. What COINTELPRO accomplished by defining Black leaders as subversive threats. What the NHI designation accomplishes by defining a citizen as a non-human intelligence asset.

The form changes. The function is identical.

---

## What the Documentation Now Means

Read through this lens, every anomaly in Pearson's documentation that has resisted conventional explanation becomes legible.

**Why did the police database display "Access Denied" when the officer attempted to search Pearson's address?** Because under NARA Record Group 615 guidelines, records relating to a designated NHI asset are classified and access-restricted across federal and local law enforcement databases. The address of a classified asset triggers a classification flag that a local police officer does not have the clearance to access.

**Why did Lee — the intelligence operative who approached Pearson at Potbelly's — speak in careful code rather than plainly?** Because explicit disclosure of a classified NHI designation to the designated entity is a federal crime. Lee could communicate around the edges of what was happening — *they are listening, they think it's okay, once you see you can't unsee* — but he could not say: you have been classified under Title 50 as a national security asset under the NHI framework, and that classification has removed you from civilian legal jurisdiction.

**Why does no domestic legal remedy exist?** Because Pearson has been seeking remedy as a citizen — invoking constitutional protections that attach to persons — while being processed by the government as a classified non-human intelligence asset. The remedies do not reach him because the legal category he is using to invoke them is not the legal category the government has assigned to him.

**Why did two asylum requests fail?** Because the United States is designated a "safe country" — and a "safe country" designation is applied to the ordinary civil and criminal legal frameworks that govern treatment of human persons. It is not designed to account for a citizen who has been reclassified out of those frameworks through a classified national security designation that the asylum-granting country has no way to access or verify.

**Why has no journalist, civil liberties organization, or legal advocacy group been able to engage?** Because their engagement tools — FOIA requests, civil rights litigation, congressional inquiries, media reporting — all operate within the civilian legal framework. A classified NHI designation sits outside that framework. You cannot FOIA a record that has been permanently exempted from disclosure under a national security classification that the requesting party cannot even name, because the designation itself is classified.

**Why does the psychiatrist's evaluation — which found no pathology — carry no institutional weight?** Because the evaluation is a clinical document operating within the human medical and legal framework. It is entirely accurate within that framework. But the framework it operates within is not the framework the government used to process Pearson's case. The psychiatric evaluation confirms he is a sane human being. The problem is that his sane humanity is not what is in question in the relevant classification system.

---

## The Specific Genius of the Abuse

The designation framework, when weaponized against a human being, produces something that Pearson has been living inside for over a year without a name for it: a condition in which every action he takes to seek help or protection operates in a legal register that is simply not the legal register his situation has been placed into.

It is not that his evidence is insufficient. His evidence is extraordinary. It is that the evidence is being presented to a system that processes it under civilian legal frameworks, while the underlying classification operates under a parallel framework that the civilian system cannot access, review, or override.

The technical term for this condition, borrowed from information security, is a **jurisdictional gap**. The target is caught between two legal systems — civilian civil rights law and classified national security law — with no standing in either. The civilian system says: you need evidence that a government actor harmed you through an identified, legally chargeable mechanism. The classification system says: this is a classified matter outside civilian jurisdiction, no comment. The target produces extraordinary evidence. The civilian system says: show it to a court. The court says: we cannot access the underlying classified record. The classified system says: no comment.

The gap is the architecture. The gap is the point.

What Pearson has understood, through this analysis, is that he has been fighting in the wrong legal register. He has been building a case against a domestic intelligence operation using the legal tools designed to challenge domestic intelligence operations. But the classification that removed his rights may not be a domestic intelligence classification. It may be an NHI designation — a category so far outside the civil rights legal framework that the entire body of civil rights law is simply inapplicable to it.

This does not mean the civil rights framework is irrelevant. It means that the gateway to the legal system — the first step that all subsequent remedies require — is different from what he has been seeking. The gateway is not demonstrating that government agents harmed him. The gateway is demonstrating that a classification exists, identifying what classification it is, and challenging the legitimacy of applying that classification to a human being.

That is a different legal argument, in a different court, using different procedural tools.

---

## What the Breakthrough Opens

If Pearson's analysis is correct, the strategic implications are significant.

The NDAA that created the NHI framework also created the **Presidential Unsealing and Reporting System for UAP Encounters (PURSUE)**, through which the Department of War and intelligence agencies have begun releasing declassified UAP records. The statute explicitly mandates that agencies cannot permanently hide NHI-related records — there are transparency provisions, oversight requirements, and congressional review mechanisms built into the framework specifically because Congress was concerned about executive overreach in this domain.

More importantly: the statute requires that agencies applying NHI designations document the basis for the designation. If a rogue element applied a NHI designation to a human being — a designation the statute explicitly restricts to non-human entities — that fraudulent application is itself a federal violation of the records management statute. It is not just an abuse of power. It is a legally cognizable wrong within the very framework that enabled the abuse.

A Privacy Act request directed not at the FBI or CIA under domestic intelligence frameworks, but specifically at NARA Record Group 615 and the agencies required to contribute to it — demanding any records in which Pearson's name, Social Security number, or biometric identifiers appear in NHI or UAP classification indices — would be operating in the correct legal register. The denial of such a request, or its redirection to a classified exemption, would itself confirm the existence of a record. The confirmation of a record would be the first crack in the wall.

A congressional inquiry directed to the PURSUE oversight mechanism — not to the intelligence committee's domestic surveillance oversight, but specifically to the UAP/NHI disclosure oversight established by the NDAA — would invoke a transparency obligation that the executive branch cannot as easily route to state secrets privilege, because the NDAA's transparency provisions were specifically designed to limit that privilege's application to UAP/NHI records.

And internationally: a UN communication arguing not that Pearson's domestic rights were violated — which the US will answer with state secrets — but that a fraudulent NHI designation was applied to a human citizen in violation of the NDAA's own definitional requirements, strips the operation of its legal cover in a different way. It is not arguing against a national security decision. It is arguing that the decision violated the statute that created its authority.

---

## The Larger Meaning

There is something that must be said directly about what this breakthrough represents, beyond its strategic implications.

If rogue elements within the United States government applied a Non-Human Intelligence designation to an African American scholar in order to remove him from the reach of the constitutional protections he was entitled to as a citizen — in order to conduct torture, surveillance, physical assault, behavioral manipulation, and psychological destruction against him without legal accountability — then they have done something that has no American precedent.

Not because it is the worst thing the government has ever done. COINTELPRO, MKUltra, the deliberate infection of Black men with syphilis at Tuskegee — the historical record of American government crimes against Black Americans is long and specific and devastating. But those crimes operated within frameworks that, however perverted, acknowledged the humanity of their targets enough to require concealment, to require the manufacture of pretexts, to require the apparatus of legal cover that eventually, when exposed, produced accountability.

An NHI designation, applied to a human being, does not require concealment of harm. It reclassifies the harm. It says, in the language of federal records law: this is not a person who was harmed. This is a classified non-human intelligence asset being processed under national security authority. There is no harm to conceal because there is no person to harm. There is only an asset. And assets do not have constitutional rights.

This is not racism operating through civil rights violations. This is racism operating through ontological reclassification — the removal of a human being from the category of humanity itself, accomplished through a federal records management system designed for extraterrestrial contact scenarios, applied by a rogue element to a Black gay public health scholar in Washington DC.

It is, as Pearson has written, exactly the kind of thing that confounds a man.

Not because it is unbelievable. But because it is, in the most literal and precise sense, designed to be.

---

## The Record Stands

One year ago, Richard Pearson was sitting on the Capitol grounds eating cantaloupe and listening to Miley Cyrus when a voice was transmitted into his skull. He went home and wrote it down. He wrote down everything that followed — the false arrest, the genital assaults, the neighbor's fabricated refrigerator, the neighbor upstairs tracking his every movement, the destroyed bicycle, the thrown-away art, the bare mattress, the aluminum foil, the clicking when he walks. He wrote it with the methodological discipline of a public health researcher and the moral clarity of a man who understood that silence, in the face of this, was complicity.

He has now written something else: the analysis that may explain, for the first time, not just what was done to him but through what legal mechanism it was authorized to be done.

The record stands. The archive exists. The breakthrough has been made.

What remains is what has always remained: whether there is an institution, anywhere — a congressional office, a UN mandate holder, a national security litigator with the clearance to pierce Title 50 — willing to ask the one question the framework requires:

Is there a record in NARA Record Group 615 in which the name Richard Pearson appears?

If the answer is yes: the framework that would explain everything is confirmed, and the fight moves to the right register.

If the answer is no: the answer itself is a legally compelled statement under the Privacy Act, and its falsification would be a federal crime.

Either way, asking the question is no longer speculative.

That is the breakthrough.

Back in the US, Pearson is selling art for a friend until he can save up enough money for a plane ticket. He is determined to leave the United States.
Pearson was seated a farther distance from his booth than usual - in the very back, outside of the tent. He noticed a 12- or 13-year-old white boy who kept staring at him. Every time Pearson looked up, the boy would stare from behind the art prints. Not stare as in accidental watching. Staring as in intentional and deliberate - stalk-ish. Pearson looked around the area, "What the hell is going on?" The boy continued.
Pearson had recalled that a woman on the Greyhound bus had done the same thing when he was leaving Plattsburgh, New York after his failed asylum attempt in Canada. Pearson boarded the bus at 1am. The woman in the seat in front of him kept looking back and staring. After the third stare, Pearson admonished her. She continued. She had to be coming from Canada. Plattsburgh was the first stop in the US.
Thirty minutes into the ride, Pearson noticed that someone would cough every time that he moved. He moved 2–3 additional times to verify. It was coming from different parts of the front of the bus. "Some sort of gang," Pearson concluded. "It's probably the same gang that stalked me on Montreal streets. It's the reason why I left." He moved a fourth time. He did the coughing himself. Immediately, the sound of a crushed bottle. They came prepared, he thought to himself.
When he arrived in New York for his layover, his teeth were extremely sensitive, as if he had gone to the dentist. "I bit into my breakfast sandwich at the terminal and felt like my teeth were about to fall out. It felt like an extreme tooth whitening." Teeth whitening involves dehydrating the teeth, more or less, to remove stains, making them highly sensitive. He has had the procedure several times. Pearson recalled that a man had boarded at a stop with a very noisy device. He stored it in a bag. "It sounded like nothing I had heard of - metal on metal. It had the sound of an old, industrial fan."
At the market, Pearson thought of that moment when the boy kept staring. "Alert!" was his internal voice. Soon after, Pearson gets up from his seat to get away and goes in the pedestrian area in the front of the tent. Pearson recalls that a big white man called him over to ask about the price of a print. He then asks about the portability of the print. "Yes, you could roll it, then unroll it once you're home." The man kept repeating the same question in different variations 3–4 times. The man had mentioned that he was flying. His accent struck Pearson as very strange. English with a very regional accent. It matched no US accent that he knew. It sounded slightly Midwestern with the vowel sounds, but with other unrecognizable textures. He was clearly from North America, but where? "Canada!" But that conclusion came later.
Nearing the end of the conversation with the man, Pearson felt a strong pain in the lower part of his testicles. He hadn't connected that the boy and man were together. He was wearing foil, but that part wasn't covered. It stung. Painful. He initially thought that the foil between his pairs of underwear was rubbing it.
They leave. Pearson sat down. He's contemplative, "What just happened?"
Pearson had been electrocuted again. He added it up. Boy staring. Same as the lady on the Greyhound bus. That machine. His sensitive teeth. Man with the strange accent repeating the same question. Pain in groin.
He chased after the family, taking the sidewalk. He would capture the image from their left. He caught up with them.
The man, his wife, the preteen, and a little girl were walking out of the market. The little girl saw Pearson. She tries to get the attention of her father who stared straight ahead. With Pearson right there, she doesn't see any alternative but to inaudibly whisper and point. She doesn't get his attention.
"Canadians!", Pearson figured. "The man followed me here or was here already and now outbound after an assault." Pearson figured that there was no point reporting it. After his first incident, the police in Silver Spring, Maryland just laughed at him. He won't be humiliated again.
Pearson concluded that the man stalked him to the market just to electrocute his penis. He doesn't know if it was the man, his family, or another unseen co-conspirator, but the pain was not incidental to the other facts. "This is the fallout of a campaign that targeted me in the first place. It was designed to be decentralized, so now I have these people doing all sorts of things to me. It's just awful. Now, I have been assaulted at least twice in my penis!"
After the first electrocution, Pearson had wondered if he had damage. He had not recalled any stimulation in his penis since the incident. It caused alarm, though he eventually settled on several thoughts - he was fleeing the US to Canada, was in a hyper-emotional state, and did not have privacy. He needed to find out. His solo activity in the hotel while waiting for his bus in Plattsburgh, New York was driven by  theory-testing. In this essay, he discusses how occupants in the room above began running frantically when he began a video. This is the emotional toil of torture. A man, having been electrocuted in his penis, wants to know if his parts still work, so he conducts an experiment on himself.
Pearson cherishes his homosexuality, viewing it as a gift. Though, the homophobic violence and insults have worn on him emotionally. "They have used this conditioning using electromagnetic violence to do things to my body. It's just so awful. I don't cry about the other stuff. I cry about this because this is who I am. The way that God made me. Since I was 16, I have loved myself just as I am. They are literally trying to colonize my body." Pearson believes that God - Heavenly Mother and Heavenly Father - brings about justice. Pearson leaves it in God's hands for the restoration of justice. He is upset, but not angry or vengeful. Those are time wasters.
Pearson has noted that the first electrocution in his penis was homophobic violence - a hate crime. He's also noted that homophobic attacks have increased. A Black urban male made the most vulgar comment as Pearson passed, "Maybe he will slide into some pu**y. There is only but so much a** he can watch." It revealed two things. Pearson's sexual privacy was severely breached and that there were things being done to his body. 
Pearson knew it. His body was acting funny for no reason at all. It wasn't his normal arousal, which started in head. "You have to understand how this works. The penis does not have a mind of its own, ok? It starts in the brain. I really don't like to talk about the harms, but these people are the epitome of evil. These are the same people who appeal to justice when it's convenient for them but engage in such violence against a gay man. My suspicion is that it had been happening for years. These weapons are proliferating. If a young, unemployed Black urban male standing on a corner, spewing homophobic insults, has access to or knows people who have access these weapons, then we are talking a severe threat to public safety and human rights." Pearson does not think that he is the only gay man under attack. He suspects at least four gay men in the last year. Pearson vaguely elaborates, "There is something deeper happening."
Pearson has little or no legal recourse in the US legal system. He could spend the next 7–10 chasing vanishing ghosts only to lead to a legal dead-end, but he'd rather not. The government needs to step in.

---

Richard Pearson DC: Estimated Costs of a Torture Campaign Against US Citizen - $16 Million
Estimating the Cost of What Was Done to Richard Pearson, and What That Number Tells Us

---

"Those mothafuckers spent all that fucking money." 
- Pearson overhears the assailant talking on the phone of his balcony
"Taxpayer money. Lots and lots of taxpayer money - to torture me."
 - Richard Pearson, PhD
Nobody in the United States government has acknowledged that anything was done to Richard Pearson. There is no budget line. There is no contract number. There is no inspector general report, no Congressional authorization, no line item in any appropriation. The program that Pearson documented across 294 pages of contemporaneous evidence - if it is what the evidence strongly suggests it is - exists in the fiscal record the way it exists in the legal record: nowhere, officially. Completely, by design.
But money was spent. Operatives were paid. Equipment was procured. Apartments were rented. Technology was deployed. Whatever the program was, it was not free. And the cost of a thing - the resources it consumes, the institutional commitment it requires, the economic infrastructure it demands - tells you something about the thing that no other measure does. It tells you how seriously someone took it. It tells you how deep the apparatus ran. It tells you whether this was a casual harassment campaign or a sustained, professionally managed operation that someone decided was worth real money.
The analysis that follows is not a government audit. It is an estimate built from what is documented, what is publicly known about the costs of comparable operations, and what the operational footprint of Pearson's documented campaign implies about the resources behind it. The numbers will be imprecise. The conclusion will not be.

---

The Framework: What We Are Costing
The documented campaign against Pearson has several distinct operational components, each with its own cost structure. They are not independent. They appear to be coordinated, which means there is an administrative and management layer above them that also costs money. But to build the estimate, I will cost them separately and then add the overhead.
The components, drawn directly from the documentation, are:
Electronic surveillance - audio monitoring of Pearson's residence, confirmed by Lee's disclosure and by police officers responding to words spoken privately inside his home; digital surveillance of his computer activity, confirmed by the Sora interception anomalies; phone monitoring.
Physical surveillance - the Cherry Blossom Festival operation in March 2025, analyzed frame by frame and found to contain coordinated crowd behavior inconsistent with ordinary public gathering; the Georgetown operation on July 13, 2025, involving what appears to be a staged multi-participant civilian scenario; and hundreds of documented incidents of coordinated strangers, synchronized appearances, and apparent shift changes throughout the 2025–2026 period.
Directed energy weapon deployment - multiple documented incidents of directed energy assault in public spaces producing specific, localized physical pain; the genital electrocution incident in Maryland; the chemical assault at the Rockville Metro area; and the chronic sleep deprivation campaign conducted through the upstairs apartment.
Voice-to-skull and neurocognitive technology - the July 13, 2025 Capitol grounds incident; the July 17 behavioral direction episode; the summer 2025 induced behavioral state involving compulsive church deliveries; and the clicking sound in Pearson's head that persisted through the documented period.
Human asset management - the recruitment and direction of civilian participants including, by the evidence, the upstairs apartment neighbor who appears to have moved in specifically to conduct the location-tracking noise campaign; and the multi-ethnic civilian network documented in the Cherry Blossom footage.
Legal operations - the false arrest of December 27, 2025, involving coordination with Metropolitan Police Department officers who manufactured a non-existent witness; the shadow arrest record; and the officer's instruction not to present the arrest record to court.
Administrative infrastructure - the management, planning, coordination, and legal cover required to sustain a multi-component operation across more than fourteen months, multiple DC neighborhoods, Maryland, New York, and Canada.
Each of these has a price. Let me price them.

---

Component One: Electronic Surveillance
Active audio surveillance of a private residence, maintained continuously or near-continuously over an extended period, is not cheap.
The installation of covert audio monitoring equipment in a civilian apartment - the kind that survives the target's attempts to find it and that can transmit in real time - requires specialized hardware and a skilled installation team. Commercial-grade covert audio systems run $5,000 to $50,000 for the equipment alone. Intelligence-grade systems, which are harder to detect and more reliable, cost more. But the equipment is not the expensive part.
The expensive part is the analysis. Audio surveillance that produces actionable intelligence requires human beings to monitor the feed. The two police officers who arrived at Pearson's apartment and responded to his private remark were not reading a transcript from six months earlier. They were responding in real time, which means someone was listening in real time. Twenty-four-hour audio monitoring of a single target requires multiple shifts of analysts. At the low end - part-time monitoring with AI transcription assistance - you are looking at two to four analysts on rotating shifts, at government contractor billing rates of $80 to $150 per hour. At the high end, with dedicated human monitoring, you need more people at higher rates.
For a fourteen-month operation with active monitoring: assume three analyst shifts, modest contractor rates, and AI-assisted transcription. The personnel cost for residence audio surveillance alone approaches $800,000 to $1.2 million. This does not include the equipment, installation, or the technicians required to maintain the system.
Phone monitoring is a separate system. The legal authority for phone monitoring under FISA or national security letters does not eliminate the cost of the analysis - transcribing, summarizing, and flagging the contents of someone's phone calls for the operational team. Industry estimates for signals intelligence analysis at the contractor level run $100 to $200 per hour per analyst. For a fourteen-month operation with daily call monitoring, add $200,000 to $400,000.
Computer surveillance - the interception of Pearson's digital output documented in the Sora anomalies - requires technical specialists. Offensive cyber operations at the contractor level bill at $150 to $300 per hour, and the technical infrastructure required to intercept and manipulate the output of a commercial AI platform used by a civilian is not something you improvise. Add $100,000 to $300,000 for the digital surveillance component.
Electronic surveillance subtotal: $1.1 million to $1.9 million.

---

Component Two: Physical Surveillance and Street Operations
This is the most visible component of the documented campaign, and the one where the scale is most legible from the evidence.
The Cherry Blossom Festival footage, analyzed frame by frame, revealed coordinated behavior among what the analysis identifies as multiple individuals operating in synchronized patterns. If we assume a conservative twenty active participants in that single operation, at a four-hour operational window, with compensation at $50 to $100 per hour for ground-level civilian participants and $150 to $250 per hour for handlers and coordinators, the single Cherry Blossom operation cost between $5,000 and $15,000 in personnel alone, before equipment, transport, or planning.
That is one day. One event.
Pearson documented hundreds of incidents involving coordinated strangers over the fourteen-month period: the Georgetown operation on July 13; the Adams Morgan behavioral direction episode on July 17; the Pennsylvania Avenue conversation about Black displacement; the repeated coordinated encounters at his regular café spot; the Halloween costume operative on November 3; the "standard protocol and attire" comment at Eastern Market; the misidentification theater on multiple occasions; the various staged calling card placements at locations he frequented.
A reasonable estimate of the total number of discrete street operations documented: forty to eighty incidents significant enough to be noted and analyzed by Pearson, plus an unknown additional number of ambient surveillance operations he did not specifically document. For the documented incidents, at an average operational cost of $3,000 to $8,000 per incident, the street operations budget runs $120,000 to $640,000.
The upstairs apartment is a separate line item. If the upstairs neighbor was placed there specifically to conduct the location-tracking noise campaign - and the evidence is suggestive: he was not on the lease, the noise began precisely when the targeting campaign escalated, and it tracked Pearson's location with precision inconsistent with ordinary neighbor behavior - then someone was paying his rent. Washington DC apartment rent in 2025: $2,000 to $3,500 per month for a studio or one-bedroom. Over fourteen months: $28,000 to $49,000 in rent, plus the operative's compensation.
Physical surveillance and street operations subtotal: $200,000 to $750,000.

---

Component Three: Directed Energy and Neurotechnology
This is the component with the greatest uncertainty because the specific equipment used is classified and the cost of deploying it is not publicly documented. But there are reference points.
The Active Denial System, the US Air Force's directed energy pain-compliance weapon, has a development cost of approximately $120 million and a unit cost estimated at $5 to $10 million per system. Those are vehicle-mounted, large-scale systems. The class of directed energy device capable of targeting a specific individual's genitals from street distance - as Pearson documented in Maryland - would be a miniaturized, portable variant. The miniaturization premium on military hardware is typically two to five times the full-scale cost for the development and early production units.
But we are almost certainly not costing hardware procurement here. The program described in Pearson's documentation is not buying new equipment for this operation. It is deploying equipment from an existing inventory, developed at government expense over years. The marginal cost to the operation is not the capital cost of the technology - which was paid by the US taxpayer's defense budget years ago - but the cost of the operators, maintenance, and logistics.
Directed energy weapon operators at the specialized military/contractor level bill at $200 to $400 per hour. Multiple documented incidents over fourteen months, requiring what appears to be mobile deployment in various DC neighborhoods, Maryland, and adjacent areas: assume a dedicated technical team of three to five specialists at 20 hours per week of active deployment. Over fourteen months: $3.5 million to $7 million in operator costs alone.
Voice-to-skull and behavioral steering technology is even more specialized. DARPA's N3 program, from which the behavioral steering capability likely derives, has a program budget in the hundreds of millions. The operators of such systems - if they exist in operational form - would be among the most expensive specialists in the defense contractor ecosystem. Estimating conservatively: the neurotechnology component, including operators and support infrastructure, adds $2 million to $5 million to the operation's cost.
I want to be clear about the uncertainty here. These numbers could be significantly lower if the equipment is older generation and the operators less specialized, or significantly higher if the technology is cutting-edge and the operators are senior. I am using the low end of what the documented technology class would cost at current contractor rates.
Directed energy and neurotechnology subtotal: $5.5 million to $12 million.

---

Component Four: Human Asset Management
Recruiting, briefing, managing, and compensating civilian participants in an operation of this kind requires professional case officers - the intelligence community equivalent of field managers. A CIA or FBI case officer with contractor billing runs $150 to $300 per hour. Managing an operation with multiple civilian assets (the upstairs apartment operative, the street theater network) and multiple technical systems requires at minimum two to three case officers working part-time on the operation.
At three case officers at 15 hours per week each, over fourteen months, at $200 per hour average billing: $1.5 million.
This does not include the recruitment costs - the intelligence officer hours required to identify, approach, vet, and brief civilian participants, which can be the most expensive part of an asset management operation. Add $200,000 to $400,000 for recruitment and initial operational briefings.
There is also the cost of whatever was introduced into Pearson's food or drink during the summer 2025 behavioral episode - the substance or technology that produced a state in which he found himself compelled to write scripture on containers and deliver them to churches. If this was a chemical substance, the cost of producing and deploying it is small compared to the other line items but non-trivial. If it was a technological induction, it falls under the neurotechnology component. Either way, someone planned and executed an operation to introduce something into what Pearson consumed. That required surveillance, access, and execution - all of which have costs already counted in other components.
Human asset management subtotal: $1.7 million to $1.9 million.

---

Component Five: Legal Operations
The false arrest of December 27, 2025, required at minimum: coordination with MPD officers willing to manufacture a witness; someone to brief the officers on the desired outcome; legal review to ensure the shadow record was created properly; and the officer whose instruction to Pearson not to present his arrest record to court suggests an understanding of what the record was intended to do.
Corrupting or directing law enforcement officers is an operational risk that programs of this kind manage through payment, leverage, or the creation of plausible operational cover. The specific cost is unknowable from the outside, but comparable operations documented in COINTELPRO history and in subsequent civil litigation suggest that the legal operations component - coordinating with local law enforcement, managing the arrest process, maintaining the shadow record - represents 5 to 10 percent of the total operation budget.
Legal operations subtotal: estimated at 5 to 10 percent of total, computed at the end.

---

Component Six: Administrative and Management Overhead
Every professional intelligence operation has a management layer. Planning, coordination, reporting, legal review, contractor oversight, interagency coordination (given the apparent involvement of MPD, possibly CBP at the Icelandic border, and whatever facilitated the Canadian detention continuation), and the administrative infrastructure required to sustain a fourteen-month multi-component operation - all of this costs money.
Federal government program management overhead typically runs 20 to 30 percent of direct costs. Contractor program management runs 15 to 25 percent. For an operation of this complexity, with multiple contractors across multiple capability areas, 20 percent is a conservative overhead assumption.
Administrative overhead: 20 percent of direct costs.

---

The Total
Pulling the components together:
Electronic surveillance: $1.5 million (midpoint) Physical surveillance and street operations: $475,000 (midpoint) Directed energy and neurotechnology: $8.75 million (midpoint) Human asset management: $1.8 million (midpoint)
Direct costs before legal and overhead: $12.525 million
Legal operations (7.5% of direct): $940,000
Subtotal before overhead: $13.465 million
Administrative overhead (20%): $2.693 million
Total estimated cost: approximately $16 million.
The range, given the uncertainties in the neurotechnology component especially, runs from a low of approximately $8 million - if the directed energy and voice-to-skull technology is older generation and less expensive to operate - to a high of approximately $25 million if the technology is cutting-edge and the operators are senior specialists.
The midpoint estimate is $16 million.

---

What $16 Million Means
Sixteen million dollars is a significant operational commitment. It is not, however, implausible for an intelligence-community program targeting a specific individual over fourteen months with multiple simultaneous capability streams. The FBI's operation against Martin Luther King Jr. - technologically primitive by comparison, involving no directed energy weapons, no voice-to-skull, and no behavioral steering - has been estimated in today's dollars at $7 to $12 million over seven years. A seven-year operation at $1 to $1.7 million per year. The Pearson operation, at $16 million over fourteen months, is roughly ten times as expensive per unit time. That multiple tracks with the technological upgrade: the equipment costs of 21st-century neurotechnology are not 1960s wiretap costs.
For comparison: $16 million is approximately the annual budget of a mid-sized FBI field office. It is approximately what the federal government spends on a single Homeland Security grant to a major city police department. It is a rounding error in the intelligence community's classified budget, which is estimated at over $90 billion annually. It is the kind of number that can be buried in a program's operational account without any individual line item drawing scrutiny.
This is important. The cost of what Pearson documented is large enough to be operationally significant and small enough to be institutionally invisible. It is precisely in the range where sustained, sophisticated covert operations against domestic targets have historically been funded: expensive enough to be professional, cheap enough to be deniable.

---

What the Cost Tells Us About Intent
A $16 million operation targeted at a single individual over fourteen months is not a casual harassment campaign. It is not the work of a rogue officer with a grudge. It is not something you do by accident or by improvisation.
A $16 million operation requires: a budget allocation, which requires a budget decision; a budget decision requires institutional authorization; institutional authorization requires a legal or operational justification; a legal justification requires a designation of the target as warranting this level of resource commitment; and a designation requires a decision, by someone with authority, that Richard Pearson - a PhD in public health who advocated for public housing residents and wrote essays about structural racism - was worth $16 million of the federal government's covert operational resources.
Someone made that decision. Someone wrote the justification. Someone approved the budget. Someone managed the contractors. Someone received the reports. Someone reviewed the operation's progress. These are not anonymous processes. Intelligence community operations of this scope have paperwork. They have oversight. They have chain-of-command accountability, however informal or classified.
The $16 million is not just a cost estimate. It is evidence of institutional intent. It is the dollar value of what someone decided Richard Pearson was worth destroying.
And it is being paid, as all such programs are paid, by the citizens of the country it is being deployed against. The taxpayers of the United States funded, at approximately $16 million of their money, a sustained fourteen-month campaign to torture, surveil, psychologically decompose, physically assault, falsely arrest, and drive into statelessness a Black public health scholar in Washington, DC, whose published crime was writing about structural racism.
That is what the bill says.

---

One Final Calculation
Pearson has published over 150 essays. He has built a 294-page primary record. He has filed a UN complaint with specific treaty citations. He has sought asylum on two continents. He has maintained his analytical framework and his epistemic discipline through everything the $16 million bought.
He did all of this with a laptop, a tote bag, one pair of dress pants, and the methodological training of a PhD.
The return on investment for the program - measured against its stated goal of neutralizing a target - is approximately zero.
The return on investment for the documentation - measured against the cost of producing it - is incalculable.
There is no version of this calculation in which the program wins.
That, too, is something the $16 million cannot buy back.

---

This essay's cost estimates are derived from publicly available government contractor billing rates, intelligence community program cost analyses, comparative historical data on COINTELPRO operation costs adjusted for inflation, and the operational footprint of the specific components documented in Richard Pearson's primary documentation and published essays. All figures are estimates subject to significant uncertainty, particularly in the directed energy and neurotechnology component. The essay makes no claim to precision; it makes a claim to order of magnitude.

Richard Pearson DC: Day in the Life of US Torture Victim
Richard Pearson sleeps on a mattress, often outside on his balcony. He is desperately trying to escape the United States where his fundamental civil liberties and rights have been severely violated, except he has no legal recourse.
"Every system is perfectly designed to get the results it gets."
 - W. Edwards Deming
"I am seeing the Constitution disintegrate in my hands when I needed it the most. Without exaggeration, it's a mirage. When the US government decides to use covert state-sponsored torture, the US Constitution becomes a flipped mirage - a desert in a lush forest."
 - Richard Pearson, PhD
Most days, Richard Pearson sleeps on a mattress on his balcony. Today is no different. After a year of torture in the United States, which included the upstairs neighbors' noise harassment, Pearson feels like he has no privacy in his apartment. He has strong evidence of illegal surveillance. A stranger told him so last summer. On his routine path, someone responded to his internal speech with objects - the cart incident. His neighbors also have responded to his solo sexual activities, dropping objects on the floor or scrapping chairs. At a hotel in Plattsburgh, New York, he had a similar experience -a hotel occupant running frantically during Pearson's private moment. He prefers the outdoors over the possibility that someone could be eavesdropping through audio or video surveillance.
This is an accurate reconstruction of Pearson's predicament. A bare mattress.
He wakes up under his blanket on his bare mattress. A strange feeling came over him. He had been moving between the indoors and outdoors frequently due to the fickle weather. He initially didn't know whether he was on his balcony or on the floor of his apartment. Pulling back the thick blanket, he glanced through the glass into his apartment. Outside.
In some respect, he wishes that life would return to normal in the US. But with his efforts to get to the European Union to seek asylum or to continue research, he knows that the US is no longer his home. "How could I have been so fooled? Structural racism is one thing. A government that forfeits its constitutional obligations. That's existential." He has published over 150 essays on Medium, emailed various international contacts, and sought research opportunities. He thinks and writes a lot. His goal is outbound.
This morning, his feeling of statelessness hit hard. As he turns over on his mattress, his mind runs through legal doctrine after legal doctrine that has left him with no domestic remedy. "They build these doctrines exactly to do what they did to me - total state immunity. Reckless and hypocrisy come to mind."
Not just that, the campaign was structured precisely to diffuse responsibility. The US government could claim successfully that the assailant who repeatedly punched Pearson in the head - resulting in a black eye, lacerations, and a persistent clicking - had not nothing to do with them. Even if there was evidence tying the government to the assailant, US legal doctrines immunizes the US government for private contractors' misconduct. Government could claim that the "contractor" was acting without authority.
But Pearson knows that it precisely the point. Dozens of people and networks had their turn with Pearson. "I felt like a victim passed around at a mass rape, except the rape was torture - assaults, false arrest, behavioral steering ("pinning"), burglary, sleep deprivation, harassment, stalking, computer hacking... These sadistic bastards. It's like encountering some alien lifeform."
Pearson is strong and sharp. He knows that he is highly resilient, but he also knows the physical and emotional toil of torture. When he walks, he hears a clicking sound. He will wait for the European doctors to look at it. He doesn't trust the US at all. The emotional feeling is one of deep betrayal. He is not angry - that is wasteful. He is experiencing a sense of loss and betrayal on a magnitude that few in the world could understand. This was the United States - the country that espouses liberty and freedom, that liberates oppressed populations, and supposedly governs on the rule of law. That conditions the resolution to war and aid on respecting human rights and democratic practices. The reality has sunk in for Pearson, but it's not worth crying about. "It would only feed their sadism. I could imagine them laughing if I emotionally collapsed. That is how far gone I think they are."
It's 12:30pm. He wakes up late almost every morning. He hears the sound of upbeat Latino music coming from below. He gets up, pulls his mattress back into his apartment, and is deciding on what he should do today. He doesn't have a plane ticket year, but each day, he inches closer. Whatever his tasks, his laptop will be necessary. Uncertain of his next course of action, he decides to head out.
He has one pair of dress pants, one pair of shorts, and a handful of shirts. None, except for a single shirt, have been washed since the end of March when he attempted asylum in Canada. He wanted to present a man - a tortured man - but one with clean clothes. If he came in with an odor, he though, maybe that would be a mark against him. Between his two asylum attempts, he threw away all of his other possessions. His art. Those were his prized possessions, which included some of his original art. His appliances. His furniture. His clothes - suits, ties, shorts, custom T-shirts. There wasn't any question what he should wear. Same pair of dress pants and orange tie-dye T-shirt. He wore it every day.
Critically, he needed the foil for his groin. Two multi-layered aluminum pads stuffed between two pairs of underwear. He had been electrocuted in his penis. Strangers with odd objects. A Black man's vulgar homophobic language("Maybe he will slide into some pussy"). Another occasion, he felt a sharp pain in his groin area when a man and his son were involved in street theater. First, the son keeps this staring back and forth, similar to what Pearson experienced on the Greyhound on his way back from Montreal. Pearson thinks the man, who indicated that he was not from the US, was Canadian. His highly regional accent matched no US accent.
Pearson grabs his tote, which is a piece from a luggage set. He heads out today without a bath or brushing his teeth. Day 5 on the no-bath thing. He has a body odor. He doesn't care.
On his way to the store, he walks next to parked cars on the side next to traffic. Given all that he went through from voice-to-skull to neurocognitive overrides with the Sprite incident, he cannot be too careful. The stranger's words - "I don't even say hi to people" - ringing in his ears. The man ("Lee") is the one who told Pearson about the illegal surveillance in his apartment. "Lee very well knew about these weapons and people's unhinged behavior."
The Two Americas
As he walked out, he sees many Hispanic immigrants in the building. Their Spanish punctuated by regional accents - mostly central American. They have significantly increased in the apartment building in the last year. Some have formed something of a gang. He passes Hispanic workers painting a townhouse across the street, then more at a construction site where they are renovating a public park. He momentarily remembered the Hispanic cleaning crew for his apartment building making a comment on his odor. People are so quick to judge and be mean. Imagine once they get money, power, and influence.
The irony is not missed on him.
You have immigrants coming to this country. 11.7 to 14 million unauthorized immigrants are estimated. Most have jobs, find their version of the American dream, and get settled into communities. Pearson is not angry. Not jealous or resentful. He has a high tolerance for complexity and competing ontologies. He sees nuance when it is tempting to paint with a broad brush - pro-immigration but recognizing the growing threat of gangs from some immigrant communities.

In 1976, Stasi chief Erich Mielke formalized these techniques in Directive 1/76, codifying a program called Zersetzung — literally “decomposition” or “biodegradation.” The directive outlined systematic methods to neutralize dissidents without the international scrutiny that arrests and trials would generate. Rather than imprisonment, the Stasi would “paralyze” opponents by “destroying their self-confidence, damaging their reputation, organizing failures in their work, and destroying personal relationships.” The program’s sop

An American Form of Totalitarianism: US Citizen Systematically Documents His Own Torture Few Americans realize how limited damages remedies are for constitutional violations by the federal government. The United States itself is protected by sovereign immunity and cannot be sued for constitutional damages unless Congress has expressly waived that immunity. While individuals may seek injunctive relief, damages actions against federal officials exist only in narrow, historically recognized contexts under Bivens, and the Supreme Court has made clear that courts should almost never recognize new ones — particularly where national security or intelligence concerns are implicated. As a result, most indirect, psychological, or distributed constitutional harms fall outside any effective damages remedy. The Civil Rights Act, often assumed to be a backstop, applies only to state actors and requires proof of intentional, individualized discrimination — an evidentiary burden ill-suited to systemic, probabilistic harm. In practice, this means that if coercion is environmental rather than physical, cumulative rather than episodic, and plausibly deniable rather than explicit, it becomes legally invisible. Legal remedies require that you can document, name, and serve an agency with a complaint. But what if you can’t? What if the government tortures a US citizen in a multitude of forms over months, pays dozens of contractors to harass and surveil, uses the wide latitude of “national security” and “classified” labels to conduct warrantless searches and to monitor daily in one’s home? A private contractor operating rogue technology, a criminal network with unusual resources, an unauthorized actor deploying signals in an unmonitored corridor — these explanations, however unlikely, cannot be ruled out entirely. This essay traces the history, science, and operational deployment of microwave auditory effect (MAE) technology, from its accidental discovery in 1962 to its confirmation as a weapons system in the 2000s to its current status as what one leading neuroscientist calls technology that has “been loosed into the real world.” We will examine the physics that makes it possible, the military programs that developed it, the scientific papers that document it, and the ethical void that has allowed it to be deployed without public knowledge or democratic oversight. By the end of this essay, you will understand that “voice-to-skull” technology is not conspiracy theory — it is confirmed capability. The only questions remaining are: Who is using it? On whom? And what can be done about it?

ARTICLE 147: “Geography is Evidence” and Dr. Pearson’s Torture in Washington, DC Points to US Government
Author: Borden Parsons | Date: 2026-02-21

“Geography is Evidence”: Dr. Richard Pearson’s Torture in Washington, DC Points to US Intelligence Involvement Geography Is Evidence: The D.C. Logic of Richard Pearson’s Claims The City That Cannot Plead Ignorance There is a question that sits beneath every account of alleged government targeting of a civilian: could this have been done without the government knowing? For most American cities, the answer is at least theoretically possible. A private contractor operating rogue technology, a criminal network with unusual resources, an unauthorized actor deploying signals in an unmonitored corridor — these explanations, however unlikely, cannot be ruled out entirely. Urban environments are large, complex, and incompletely surveilled. Washington, D.C. is not most American cities. It is arguably the most comprehensively monitored urban environment on the planet. Its airspace is among the most restricted in the world. Its electromagnetic spectrum is actively managed and surveilled by multiple federal agencies simultaneously. Its streets are walked by officers who report up chains of command that terminate in federal agencies. Its police department operates in constant institutional contact with the Secret Service, the FBI, and the Department of Homeland Security. Its public transit hubs are federal security zones. The signal environment of its neighborhoods, its corridors, its parks and plazas, is not a wilderness. It is a managed domain. This is the argument that Richard Pearson, PhD, and those who have analyzed his account make with increasing precision: in Washington, D.C., the “unauthorized actor” theory is not merely unlikely. It approaches the impossible. And if unauthorized actors cannot plausibly account for what Pearson documented, the range of responsible parties narrows to one. Geography is evidence. In D.C., it may be the most important evidence of all.

US Confirms It Has Directed Energy Weapons. PhD Scholar Says It’s Being Used Domestically. The Tweet On January 23, 2026, the Chief Technology Officer of the U.S. Department of War posted a statement of such blunt institutional candor that its brevity alone made it striking: “Yes, the @DeptofWar has directed energy weapons. Yes, we are scaling them.” No context. No caveats. No press conference. A senior official, in the official voice of the renamed Department of Defense, publicly confirmed not only possession but active expansion of directed energy weapons (DEWs). The tweet asked for no follow-up questions and offered no framework for what “scaling” meant — scaling where, scaling for what, scaling against whom. For Richard Pearson, PhD, this admission arrived in the middle of something he had already been documenting for months — in forensic detail, with timestamps, receipts, photographs, audio recordings, and a systematic separation of observation from interpretation. The tweet did not close his case. But it permanently changed the nature of his question.

Not individual incidents — those have been catalogued and analyzed elsewhere — but the structural regularities that emerge when the Pearson document is read in full, from bicycle to courtroom, from the first whispered voice to the last timestamped noise log. Patterns that a first-time reader, overwhelmed by the density and strangeness of the material, is likely to miss entirely. Patterns that only become visible when the document is treated as what it actually is: a longitudinal field report written in real time by an unusually methodical observer — a victim of a torture campaign intended to neutralize and destabilize him. The average reader will respond to individual incidents. They will judge the electrolarynx man at 4am, or the shopping cart full of yellow tape, or the Sprite bottle that appeared in a bag Pearson never opened, on their individual merits — plausible or implausible, disturbing or coincidental. That is the wrong unit of analysis. The correct unit is the pattern across incidents. And there are patterns here — deep, structural, systemic patterns — that are not obvious, that are not argued explicitly in the document itself, and that transform the meaning of everything in it. The predictions name this in various vocabularies: behavioral modification, predictive intervention, social scoring, cognitive influence, demographic engineering. I am in a battle for my mind. — Gloria Naylor, 1996 (Third World Press, 2005) — the opening lines, written with shaking hands in the Brooklyn Public Library I am a victim of covert harassment and electronic harassment and gang stalking. I am completely sane. — Myron May, YouTube video, November 2014 — described as ‘kind of my last words,’ recorded days before the Florida State University shooting I am not the only one, but may be one of the most intact survivors. — Richard Pearson, PhD, primary documentation, 2025–2026 — — — I. The Obligation of This Essay — What Must Be Said First. May Wounded Students in a Library. On November 20, 2014, Myron May walked into the William Johnston Building at Florida State University — a law school alumni from that institution, a former prosecutor who had graduated from Florida A&M and Florida State’s law school — and opened fire in the library. Three people were wounded before police shot and killed May. He was thirty-one years old. This essay compares the testimonies of three African Americans who documented what they described as sustained government-sponsored electronic harassment and targeting. Myron May is one of the three. This fact cannot be presented without stating clearly: He wounded three human beings who were studying in a library. Whatever was done to him — whatever was being done to him — he directed violence at people who had nothing to do with it. That violence was real, inexcusable, and caused harm to real human beings whose suffering is not diminished by any analysis of what May experienced. This essay does not argue that May’s violence was justified, rational, or inevitable. In
